Meanwhile, the Baron, with the remainder of the huntsmen, had ridden up to the water’s edge, and his anger was in an instant converted into grief and alarm as soon as he became aware of the perilous situation of his pricker; for the Lord of Vez had a sincere love towards those who ministered to his pleasure, whether man or beast. In a loud voice he shouted to his followers: “By all the powers of hell! Save Marcotte! Five and twenty louis, fifty louis, a hundred louis, to anyone who will save him!” And men and horses, like so many startled frogs, leaped into the water, vying with each other who should be first. The Baron was for riding into the river himself, but his henchmen held him back, and so anxious were they to prevent the worthy Baron from carrying out his heroic intention, that their affection for their master was fatal to the poor pricker. For one moment he was forgotten, but that last moment meant his death. He appeared once more above the surface, just where the river makes a bend; he was seen to battle against the water, and his face for an instant rose into view, as with one last cry he called to his hounds, “Back! dogs, back!” But the water again closed over him, stifling the last syllable of the last word, and it was not till a quarter of an hour later that his body was found lying on a little beach of sand on to which the current had washed him. Marcotte was dead; there was no doubt about it! This accident was disastrous in its effect on the Lord of Vez. Being the noble lord he was, he had somewhat of a liking for good wine; and this predisposed him ever so little to apoplexy, and now, as he came face to face with the corpse of his good servitor, the emotion was so great, that the blood rushed to his head and brought on a fit.

===Chinese===
与此同时，男爵带着余下的猎手已策马赶到水边，一瞧见他那猎犬长命悬一线，满腔怒火霎时化作了悲恸与惊惶——因为韦兹领主对凡是为他效劳取乐的人与畜，都有一份真切的情分。他扯开嗓门冲着随从们高喊：“凭地狱里所有的鬼神！救马尔科特！二十五路易（louis），五十路易，一百路易，谁救了他，这钱就赏谁！”于是人和马，像一群受了惊的青蛙，纷纷扑通扑通跳进水里，争先恐后地要比个先后。男爵本人也要纵马下河，却被他手下的亲随死死拉住，他们一心要拦住这位好男爵、不让他去逞那一腔子英雄气概，这份为主子的忠心，反倒要了那可怜猎犬长的命。有一忽儿工夫他被人抛在了脑后，可就是这最后的一忽儿，便断送了他的性命。他在河流拐弯的地方又冒出水面一次；人们瞧见他在水里挣扎，他的脸有一瞬探出水面，只听他用尽最后一口气朝他的猎犬高喊：“回来！狗，回来！”可水又合拢过来，淹没了他最后那个字最后一个音节。直到一刻钟之后，人们才寻见他的尸首，横陈在一小片沙滩上，是被水流冲上岸的。马尔科特死了；这是千真万确的！这一桩意外，落在韦兹领主身上，后果之惨，不堪设想。他既是这么一位高贵的老爷，便不免有些贪杯好酒的嗜好；这点嗜好叫他略微有些中风的底子，如今他直面这位忠仆的尸首，悲恸之情排山倒海般涌上来，热血直冲头顶，当场便发了中风，昏倒在地。

===Original===
Thibault felt appalled as he realised with what scrupulous exactness the black wolf had fulfilled his part of the contract, and not without a shudder did he think of the right Master Isengrin now had to claim an equal punctuality of payment in return. He began to wonder uneasily whether the wolf, after all, was the kind of being that would continue to be satisfied with a few hairs﻿—and this the more that both at the moment of his wish and during the succeeding minutes during which it was being accomplished, he had not been conscious of the slightest sensation anywhere about the roots of his hair, not even of the least little tickling. He was far from being pleasantly affected by the sight of poor Marcotte’s corpse; he had not loved him, it is true, and he had felt that he had good reason for not doing so; but his dislike to the defunct had never gone so far as to make him wish for his death, and the wolf had certainly gone far beyond his desires. At the same time, Thibault had never precisely said what he did wish, and had left the wolf a wide margin for the exercise of his malice; evidently he would have to be more careful in future in stating exactly what he wanted, and above all, more circumspect as regards any wish he might formulate.

===Chinese===
蒂博一旦认清那黑狼履行契约的字字句句竟这般一丝不苟、毫厘不爽，登时觉得心惊胆战；再一想到伊森格兰先生（Master Isengrin）这会儿便有了同等的权利，反过来向他索取同样准时的偿付，他便止不住一阵寒噤。他不由得惴惴不安地盘算起来：那狼到底是不是一种肯长久满足于区区几根头发的角色——更何况，不论是在他发愿的那一刹那，还是在愿望正在兑现的随后那几分钟里，他发根上上下下都觉察不出一星半点异样的感觉，连最细微的一点酥痒都没有。他瞧着可怜马尔科特的尸首，心里断谈不上舒坦；不错，他并不爱这个人，他也自觉有十足的理由不爱他；可他对死者的嫌恶，从未走到要他性命这一步，那狼分明已远远超出了他的本意。可话说回来，蒂博原也未曾把自己到底要什么说得清清楚楚，反倒给那狼留下偌大的余地，任它施展恶毒的心思；往后再发愿，显然他得格外当心，把所求之事交代得明明白白，尤其要字斟句酌，三思而后许。

===Original===
As to the Baron, although still alive, he was almost as good as dead. From the moment when, as the result of Thibault’s wish, he had been struck down as it were by lightning, he had remained unconscious. His men had laid him on the heap of heather which the shoemaker had piled up to hide the door of the shed, and troubled and frightened, were ransacking the place to try and find some restorative which might bring their master back to life. One asked for vinegar to put on his temples, another for a key to put down his back, this one for a bit of board to slap his hands with, that for some sulphur to burn under his nose. In the midst of all this confusion was heard the voice of little Engoulevent, calling out: “In the name of all that’s good, we don’t want all this truck, we want a goat. Ah! if only we had a goat!”

“A goat?” cried Thibault, who would have rejoiced to see the Baron recover, for it would lift at least part of the burden now weighing on his conscience, and would also rid his dwelling of these marauders. “A goat? I have a goat!”

“Really! you have a goat?” cried Engoulevent, “oh! my friends! now our dear master is saved!”

And so overcome with joy was he, that he flung his arms round Thibault’s neck, saying, “Bring out your goat, my friend! bring out your goat!”

Thibault went to the shed and led out the goat, which ran after him bleating.

“Hold it firmly by the horns,” said the huntsman, “and lift up one of its front feet.” And as he gave the word, the second huntsman drew from its sheath a little knife which he carried in his belt, and began carefully sharpening it on the grindstone which Thibault used for his tools. “What are you going to do?” asked the shoemaker, feeling somewhat uneasy about these preparations.

“What! don’t you know,” said Engoulevent, “that there is a little bone in the shape of a cross inside a goat’s heart, which, if crushed into powder, is a sovereign remedy for apoplexy?”

“You intend to kill my goat?” exclaimed Thibault, at the same time leaving hold of the goat’s horns, and dropping its foot, “but I will not have it killed.”

“Fie, fie!” said Engoulevent, “that is not at all a becoming speech, Monsieur Thibault, would you value the life of our good master as of no more worth than that of your wretched goat? I am truly ashamed for you.”

“It’s easy for you to talk. This goat is all I have to depend upon, the only thing I possess. She gives me milk, and I am fond of her.”

“Ah! Monsieur Thibault, you cannot be thinking of what you are saying﻿—it is fortunate that the Baron does not hear you﻿—for he would be brokenhearted to know that his precious life was being bargained for in that miserly way.”

“And besides,” said one of the prickers with a sneering laugh, “if Master Thibault values his goat at a price which he thinks only my lord can pay, there is nothing to prevent him coming to the castle of Vez to claim this payment. The account can be settled with what was left over as due to him yesterday.”

===Chinese===
至于男爵，虽说还活着，却已与死人无异。自从他因蒂博那一愿，如同遭了雷殛似的栽倒在地，便一直人事不省。手下人把他抬到鞋匠为遮掩披屋门而堆起的那堆石楠花上，又急又怕，在屋里四处乱翻，想找点什么提神的药，好把主人救活过来。这一个讨醋来要给他擦太阳穴，那一个要一把钥匙顺着脊背往下放，这个要一块板子来拍他的手心，那个要点硫磺凑到他鼻底下烧。正乱作一团的当儿，只听得小个子昂古勒旺（Engoulevent）的嗓门嚷了起来：“凭一切圣贤的名！我们不要这堆破烂，我们要一头山羊。啊！要是咱们有头山羊就好了！”

“一头山羊？”蒂博叫道——他巴不得男爵醒过来，因为这样一来，他良心上这副重担至少能卸去一半，再者也能把这些打劫的人从他屋里打发走。“一头山羊？我有山羊！”

“当真！你有山羊？”昂古勒旺嚷道，“噢！朋友们！咱们亲爱的老爷有救啦！”

他欢喜得什么似的，竟一把搂住蒂博的脖子，说道：“把你的山羊牵出来吧，我的朋友！把你的山羊牵出来吧！”

蒂博走进披屋，把那头山羊牵了出来，山羊咩咩叫着，跟在他身后跑。

“抓住它的角，攥牢了。”猎手说道，“再把它一只前蹄抬起来。”话音刚落，第二个猎手便从腰间所佩刀鞘里拔出一柄小刀，在蒂博磨工具用的那块磨刀石上仔仔细细地磨起来。“你们要做什么？”鞋匠问道，瞧着这番布置，心里老大不踏实。

“怎么！你不知道吗，”昂古勒旺说道，“山羊的心里头有一块十字形的小骨头，磨成粉末，便是治中风的圣药？”

“你们要杀我的山羊？”蒂博失声叫道，一边松开了攥着羊角的手，放下了那只蹄子，“可我不许你们杀它。”

“啧啧！”昂古勒旺说道，“这话可太不中听了，蒂博先生，难道您把我们好老爷的性命看得还不如您那头可怜的山羊值钱？我真替您害臊。”

“你说得倒轻巧。这头山羊是我全部的家当，是我仅有的一点东西。它给我奶喝，我心疼它。”

“啊！蒂博先生，您这会儿准是不知道自己在说什么——幸亏男爵听不见您这话——他若是知道人家竟这般小家子气地讨价还价来换他这条金贵的命，非心碎不可。”

“再说了，”一个猎犬长冷笑着插嘴道，“倘若蒂博先生把他这头山羊看得这般金贵，以为只有老爷才出得起这个价，那也不妨请他自到韦兹（Vez）城堡去讨这笔账。这笔款子，尽可拿昨天还欠着他的那笔账来抵。”

===Original===
Thibault knew that he could not get the better of these men, unless he again called the devil to his aid; but he had just received such a lesson from Satan, that there was no fear of his exposing himself, at all events for a second time the same day, to similar good offices. His one desire for the time being was not to wish any sort of ill to anyone of those present.

One man dead, another nearly so﻿—Thibault found this lesson enough. Consequently, he kept his eyes turned away from the menacing and jeering countenances around him, for fear of being aggravated beyond control. While his back was turned, the poor goat’s throat was cut, her piteous cry alone informing him of the fact; and it was no sooner killed than its heart, which had hardly ceased throbbing, was opened in search of the little bone of which Engoulevent had spoken. This found, it was ground into powder, mixed with vinegar diluted with thirteen drops of gall from the bladder containing it, the whole stirred together in a glass with the cross of a rosary, and then poured gently down the Baron’s throat, after his teeth had been forced apart with the blade of a dagger.

The effect of the draught was immediate and truly miraculous. The Lord of Vez sneezed, sat up, and said in a voice, intelligible though still a little husky: “Give me something to drink.”

Engoulevent handed him some water in a wooden drinking-cup, a family possession, of which Thibault was very proud. But the Baron had no sooner put his lips to it and become aware of what the vile, abominable liquid was, which they had had the impudence to offer him, than, with an exclamation of disgust, he flung the vessel and its contents violently against the wall, and the cup fell, smashed into a thousand pieces. Then in a loud and sonorous voice, which left no doubt of his perfect recovery, he called out: “Bring me some wine.” One of the prickers mounted and rode at full speed to the castle of Oigny, and there requested the lord of the place to give him a flask or two of sound old Burgundy; ten minutes after he was back again. Two bottles were uncorked, and there being no glasses at hand, the Baron put them in turn to his mouth, draining each at a single draught.

Then he turned himself round with his face to the wall, and murmuring﻿—Mâcon, 1743﻿—fell into a profound slumber.

===Chinese===
蒂博心知，这一干人他是占不了上风的，除非他再去请魔鬼相助；可他方才刚从撒旦那里得了这么一个教训，断不至于——至少在同一天里不至——再把自己送去领受类似的“好意”了。他眼下唯一的指望，便是对在场诸人，一个也不愿再降下半点祸事。

死了一个，又一个半死不活——蒂博觉得这一个教训已经尽够了。于是他把眼光从周遭那一张张威吓的、讥诮的脸上移开，生怕再看一眼便要按捺不住心头火起。趁他背过身去，那可怜的山羊便被割了喉，唯有它一声哀啼，才叫他知晓了这事；山羊刚一断气，那颗尚在怦怦乱跳的心便被剖了开来，去找昂古勒旺所说的那块小骨头。找到了，便磨成粉末，和上醋，再兑进从胆里取出的十三滴胆汁，将这调和之物放在一只玻璃杯里，拿一串念珠上的十字架搅了又搅，而后撬开男爵的牙关——是用一柄匕首的刀刃撬的——将药汁缓缓灌进他的喉咙里去。

这服药的效力立竿见影，简直堪称神迹。韦兹领主打了个喷嚏，坐起身来，开口说道——嗓音虽说还有些沙哑，字字句句却听得明白：“给我一点喝的。”

昂古勒旺拿一只木制饮杯盛了水递给他——这饮杯是蒂博家传的旧物，蒂博一直引以为豪。可男爵嘴唇才一沾那杯口，辨出他们竟敢拿这等污浊不堪的液体来孝敬他，便厌恶地低呼一声，将杯子和杯里的水狠狠朝墙上一甩，杯子落下地来，摔得粉碎。紧接着，他用洪亮浑厚的嗓门——这一嗓子，足以叫人对他的痊愈不再有半点疑心——高声吩咐道：“拿酒来。”一个猎犬长翻身上马，全速驰往瓦尼（Oigny）城堡，请那里的领主给他一两瓶上好的陈年勃艮第（Burgundy）；不出十分钟便赶了回来。拔开两瓶酒的塞子，手边又没有杯子，男爵便把酒瓶轮流举到嘴边，一瓶一口，一饮而尽。

随后他转过身去，脸朝着墙，嘴里喃喃念着——Mâcon，1743——便沉沉睡去了。

## II The Seigneur Jean and the Sabot-Maker / II 让领主与木屐匠

===Original===
As already said, the buck began to dodge and double on reaching Oigny, turning and twisting round Thibault’s hut, and the weather being fine although the autumn was well advanced, the shoemaker was sitting at his work in his open lean-to. Looking up, he suddenly espied the trembling animal, quivering in every limb, standing a few paces in front of him, gazing at him with intelligent and terrified eyes.

Thibault had been for a long time aware that the hunt was circling around Oigny, at one time drawing near to the village, and then receding, only to draw near again.

There was nothing therefore very surprising to him in the sight of the buck, yet he stayed his hand, although he was busy at work, and contemplated the animal.

===Chinese===
如前所述，那头雄鹿一跑到瓦尼（Oigny）便开始左躲右闪，围着蒂博（Thibault）的小屋兜来绕去。其时深秋已至，天气却仍晴好，鞋匠正坐在他那敞亮的披屋里做活。他一抬头，猛然瞧见那头瑟瑟发抖的畜生就在眼前几步开外站着，四肢抖个不住，用一双既聪慧又惊恐的眼睛望着他。

蒂博早就觉察到这场围猎在瓦尼四周兜圈子，一时逼近村庄，一时又远去，转瞬又再度逼近。

因此，瞧见这头雄鹿他并不觉得怎么稀奇，可他还是停下了手——虽说活计正忙——端详起这畜生来。

===Original===
“Saint Sabot!” he exclaimed—I should explain, that the festival of Saint Sabot is the wooden-shoe fête—“Saint Sabot! but that is a dainty morsel and would taste as fine, I warrant, as the chamois I ate at Vienne once at the grand banquet of the Jolly Shoemakers of Dauphiné. Lucky folk who can dine on the like every day. I tasted such once, it is now nearly four years ago, and my mouth waters now when I think of it. Oh! these lords! these lords! with their fresh meats and their old wines at every meal, while I have to be satisfied with potatoes to eat and water to drink from one week’s end to the other; and it is a chance if even on Sunday, I can feast myself with a lump of rusty bacon and an old cabbage, and a glass of *pignolet* fit to make my old goat stand on her head.”

===Chinese===
“木鞋圣徒保佑！”他叫出声来——我得说明一句，木鞋圣徒节（the festival of Saint Sabot）便是木鞋匠人的守护节日——“木鞋圣徒保佑！这可真是块肥嫩的好肉，吃进嘴里保管跟我那回在维埃纳（Vienne）多菲内（Dauphiné）快活鞋匠公会的那场大宴上吃到的岩羚羊肉一般鲜美。那些有福之人，天天都能吃上这样的好东西。我也曾尝过一回那样的滋味，至今快有四年了，如今一想起来嘴里还直淌口水。噢！这些老爷！这些老爷！顿顿吃的是鲜肉，喝的是陈酿，我却只能从这礼拜末熬到下礼拜末，啃土豆、喝清水；就算是礼拜天，能啃上一块生了霉的咸肉、一颗老白菜，再灌上一杯酸得能让我那头老母羊拿大顶的 *pignolet* 粗酒，便得算是撞了大运了。”

===Original===
It need scarcely be said, that as soon as Thibault began this monologue, the buck had turned and disappeared. Thibault had finished rounding his periods, and had just declaimed his peroration, when he heard himself roughly accosted in forcible terms:

“Ho, there, you scoundrel! answer me.”

It was the Baron, who seeing his dogs wavering, was anxious to make sure that they were not on the wrong scent.

“Ho, there, you scoundrel!” repeated the wolf-hunter, “have you seen the beast?”

===Chinese===
几乎不用多说，蒂博这番独白才一开口，那头雄鹿便已掉头溜走了。蒂博的抑扬顿挫方才念罢，那套高谈阔论刚作收尾，便听得有人粗声恶气地冲他喝问起来：

“喂，那边那个，你这无赖！回我的话。”

来人正是男爵（the Baron），他见猎犬们犹疑不定，急于弄清楚它们是不是嗅错了气味。

“喂，那边那个，你这无赖！”这位猎狼人又重复了一遍，“你瞧见那畜生了没有？”

===Original===
There was evidently something in the manner of the Baron’s questioning which did not please our philosophical shoemaker, for although he was perfectly aware what was the matter, he answered: “what beast?”

“Curse you! why, the buck we are hunting! He must have passed close by here, and standing gaping as you do, you must have seen him. It was a fine stag of ten, was it not? Which way did he go? Speak up, you blackguard, or you shall have a taste of my stirrup-leather!”

“The black plague take him, cub of a wolf!” muttered the shoemaker to himself.

Then, aloud, with a fine air of pretended simplicity, “Ah, yes!” he said, “I did see him.”

===Chinese===
男爵这问话的腔调里，分明有什么叫我们这位达观的鞋匠听着不受用，因为尽管他心里清清楚楚是怎么回事，嘴上却回道：“什么畜生？”

“该死的！还用问，就是我们正在追的那头雄鹿！它准是从这附近过去的，你这么傻站着张着大嘴，不可能没瞧见它。那是头十叉的壮鹿，是不是？它往哪边跑了？快说，你这无赖，不然叫你尝尝我马镫皮的滋味！”

“黑瘟病带走他吧，这狼崽子！”鞋匠暗自咕哝道。

随后他提高嗓门，装出一副傻呵呵的憨相：“啊，是啦！”他说，“我是瞧见它了。”

===Original===
“A buck, was it not? a ten-tiner, eh? with great horns.”

“Ah, yes to be sure, a buck, with great horns—or great corns, was it? yes, I saw him as plain as I see you, my Lord. But there, I can’t say if he had any corns, for I did not look at his feet, anyhow,” he added, with the air of a perfect simpleton, “if he had corns, they did not prevent him running.”

At any other time the Baron would have laughed at what he might have taken for genuine stupidity; but the doublings of the animal were beginning to put him into a regular huntsman’s fever.

“Now, then, you scoundrel, a truce to this jesting! If you are in a humour for jokes, it is more than I am!”

===Chinese===
“是头雄鹿，是不是？十叉的，嗯？长着大大的角。”

“啊，没错没错，是头雄鹿，长着大角——还是大鸡眼来着，是吗？是的，我瞧它瞧得清清楚楚，就像我瞧见老爷您一样清楚。不过嘛，我可说不上它有没有鸡眼，因为我没去看它的脚，反正，”他补了一句，一副十足呆子的神气，“就算它长了鸡眼，也碍不着它撒腿跑啊。”

换作别的时候，男爵定会把这当作真情实意的犯傻而哈哈大笑；可这会儿那畜生东绕西拐，已叫他犯起猎人所特有的那一股子急火来了。

“行了，你这无赖，少跟我打哈哈！你要是有兴致说笑话，我可没那个兴致！”

===Original===
“I will be in whatever humour it may please your Lordship I should be.”

“Well, then, answer me.”

“Your Lordship has asked me nothing as yet.”

“Did the deer seem tired?”

“Not very.”

“Which way did he come?”

“He did not come, he was standing still.”

“Well, but he must have come from one side or the other.”

“Ah! very likely, but I did not see him come.”

“Which way did he go?”

“I would tell you directly; only I did not see him go.”

===Chinese===
“老爷要我什么心境，我便是什么心境。”

“那就给我回话。”

“老爷您还什么都没问我呢。”

“那鹿瞧着累不累？”

“不算太累。”

“它是从哪一边来的？”

“它不是来的，它原本就站着不动。”

“也罢，可它总归是从这一边或那一边来的。”

“啊！多半是吧，可我没瞧见它来。”

“那它往哪一边去了？”

“我这就告诉您；只可惜我没瞧见它走。”

===Original===
The Lord of Vez cast an angry look at Thibault.

“Is it some while ago the buck passed this way, Master Simpleton?”

“Not so very long, my Lord.”

“About how long ago?”

Thibault made as if trying to remember; at last he replied:

“It was, I think, the day before yesterday,” but in saying this, the shoemaker, unfortunately, could not suppress a grin. This grin did not escape the Baron, who, spurring his horse, rode down on Thibault with lifted whip.

===Chinese===
韦兹领主（the Lord of Vez）恶狠狠地瞪了蒂博一眼。

“那鹿打这儿过去有多久了，傻大哥？”

“不算太久，老爷。”

“大约多久？”

蒂博做出一副努力回想的样子；末了才答道：

“我想，怕是前天吧。”可不幸的是，鞋匠说这话时，到底没忍住咧嘴一笑。这一笑没能逃过男爵的眼睛，他一夹马刺，举着马鞭便朝蒂博冲了过来。

===Original===
Thibault was agile, and with a single bound he reached the shelter of his lean-to, whither the wolf-hunter could not follow, as long as he remained mounted; Thibault was therefore in momentary safety.

“You are only bantering and lying!” cried the huntsman, “for there is Marcassino, my best hound, giving cry not twenty yards off, and if the deer passed by where Marcassino is, he must have come over the hedge, and it is impossible, therefore, that you did not see him.”

“Pardon, my Lord, but according to our good priest, no one but the Pope is infallible, and Monsieur Marcassino may be mistaken.”

“Marcassino is never mistaken, do you hear, you rascal! and in proof of it I can see from here the marks where the animal scratched up the ground.”

“Nevertheless, my Lord, I assure you, I swear.…” said Thibault, who saw the Baron’s eyebrows contracting in a way that made him feel uneasy.

===Chinese===
蒂博身手矫健，纵身一跃便钻进了披屋的遮蔽之下——只要猎手还骑在马上，便追不进这地方来；因此蒂博眼下算是暂且安全了。

“你不过是在拿我开涮、撒谎罢了！”猎人嚷道，“因为我那条最得力的猎犬马尔卡西诺（Marcassino），就在不出二十码的地方吠叫呢，要是那鹿打马尔卡西诺那儿经过，它就必是翻过篱笆来的，因此你不可能没瞧见它。”

“恕罪，老爷，可照我们那位好神父说，除了教皇谁都不可能一贯正确，马尔卡西诺先生说不定也弄错了呢。”

“马尔卡西诺从来不会弄错，听见没有，你这泼皮！为作证明，我打这儿就能瞧见那畜生刨地的爪印。”

“话虽如此，老爷，我向您保证，我发誓……”蒂博说道，他眼见男爵的双眉越锁越紧，那副模样叫他心里发毛。

===Original===
“Silence, and come here, blackguard!” cried my lord.

Thibault hesitated a moment, but the black look on the sportsman’s face became more and more threatening, and fearing to increase his exasperation by disobeying his command, he thought he had better go forward, hoping that the Baron merely wished to ask a service of him.

But it was an unlucky move on his part, for scarcely had he emerged from the protection of the shed, before the horse of the Lord of Vez, urged by bit and spur, gave a leap, which brought his rider swooping down upon Thibault, while at the same moment a furious blow from the butt end of the Baron’s whip fell upon his head.

===Chinese===
“闭嘴，给我过来，你这无赖！”老爷喝道。

蒂博迟疑了片刻，可这猎人脸上的阴沉之色越发吓人，他生怕不从命更添对方的怒火，便想着还是上前为好，指望男爵不过是要差他办件事罢了。

可他这一动却是失算，因为他才从披屋的遮蔽下探出身子，韦兹领主的坐骑便受嚼子与马刺一催，纵身一跃，将骑手如鹰隼般扑到蒂博跟前，与此同时，男爵那马鞭的粗柄狠狠一击，已落在了他的头上。

===Original===
The shoemaker, stunned by the blow, tottered a moment, lost his balance and was about to fall face downwards, when the Baron, drawing his foot out of the stirrup, with a violent kick in the chest, not only straightened him again, but sent the poor wretch flying in an opposite direction, where he fell with his back against the door of his hut.

“Take that!” said the Baron, as he first felled Thibault with his whip, and then kicked him, “take that for your lie, and that for your banter!”

And then, without troubling himself any further about the man, whom he left lying on his back, the Lord of Vez, seeing that the hounds had rallied on hearing Marcassino’s cry, gave them a cheery note on his horn, and cantered away.

===Chinese===
鞋匠被这一击打得眼冒金星，踉踉跄跄晃了一晃，失了平衡，正要面朝下栽倒在地，男爵却从马镫里抽出脚来，照他胸口狠狠一蹬——这一脚不但把他踹得直挺挺地立了起来，更把这条可怜虫踢得朝相反的方向飞了出去，脊背狠狠撞在小屋的门板上。

“给你这个！”男爵说，他先用马鞭把蒂博打翻，继而又一脚将他踹飞，“这一下是赏你的谎话，这一下是赏你的贫嘴！”

说罢，他也不再理会这个仰面朝天躺在地上的汉子，韦兹领主一见猎犬们听见马尔卡西诺的吠声已重新聚拢，便朝它们吹了一支轻快的号角，策马小跑而去。

===Original===
Thibault lifted himself up, feeling bruised all over, and began feeling himself from head to foot to make sure that no bones were broken.

Having carefully passed his hand over each limb in succession, “that’s all right,” he said, “there is nothing broken either above or below, I am glad to find. So, my Lord Baron, that is how you treat people, because you happen to have married a Prince’s bastard daughter! But let me tell you, my fine fellow, it is not you who will eat the buck you are hunting today; it will be this blackguard, this scoundrel, this simpleton of a Thibault who will eat it. Yes, it shall be I who eat it, that I vow!” cried Thibault, confirming himself more and more in his bold resolution, and it is no use being a man if having once made a vow, one fails to keep it.

===Chinese===
蒂博撑着爬起来，觉得周身骨头都像散了架似的疼，便从头到脚把自个儿摸了一遍，看看有没有摔断骨头。

他把每一处肢节依次仔仔细细地摸过一遍之后，说道：“还好，浑身上下里里外外，高兴地发觉一样也没断。好啊，男爵老爷，您就因为娶了一位亲王的私生女，便这般作践人！可我告诉您吧，我的好老爷，今儿您追的那头鹿，吃它的绝不是您；要吃它的，是这个无赖、这个恶棍、这个蒂博大傻瓜。没错，吃它的就是我，我发这个誓！”蒂博嚷道，越发坚定了他这胆大包天的主意——既然立了誓却不遵守，那还算什么堂堂男儿。

===Original===
So without further delay, Thibault thrust his billhook into his belt, seized his boar-spear, and after listening for a moment to the cry of the hounds to ascertain in which direction the hunt had gone, he ran off with all the speed of which a man’s legs are capable to get the start of them, guessing by the curve which the stag and its pursuers were following what would be the straight line to take so as to intercept them.

There were two ways of doing his deed open to Thibault; either to hide himself beside the path which the buck must take and kill him with his boar-spear, or else to surprise the animal just as he was being hunted down by the dogs, and collar him there and then.

And as he ran, the desire to revenge himself on the Baron for the latter’s brutality, was not so uppermost in Thibault’s mind as the thoughts of the sumptuous manner in which he would fare for the next month, on the shoulders, the back, and the haunches of the deer, either salted to a turn, roasted on the spit, or cut in slices and done in the pan. And these two ideas, moreover, of vengeance and gluttony, were so jumbled up in his brain, that while still running at the top of his speed he laughed in his sleeve, as he pictured the dejected mien of the Baron and his men returning to the castle after their fruitless day’s hunt, and at the same time saw himself seated at table, the door securely fastened, and a pint of wine beside him, tête-à-tête with a haunch of the deer, the rich and delicious gravy escaping as the knife returned for a third or fourth cut.

===Chinese===
于是蒂博不再耽搁，把钩镰往腰带里一插，抓起他的野猪矛，侧耳听了一听猎犬的吠声，辨明围猎的去向，便撒开两条腿，以人腿所能及的全速飞奔而去，抢到他们前头——他凭那头鹿与追兵所兜的弧线，推算出截住他们该走的直线距离。

摆在蒂博面前的法子有两条：一是埋伏在那头雄鹿必经的小径旁，用野猪矛将它戳翻；二是趁那畜生被猎犬追得走投无路之时出其不意，当场将它拿下。

他一边跑，一边盘算着要为男爵的暴行报复，可这念头比起他心头对往后一个月如何大快朵颐的想望，倒还算不上头一桩——那鹿的肩胛、脊背和后腿，腌得恰到好处的，叉在烤叉上烤的，切成片下锅煎的，样样都叫人垂涎。况且，复仇与贪嘴这两桩心思在他脑子里搅作一团，他虽还在拼了命地飞跑，却已忍不住暗自窃笑：他一面想象着男爵和他手下的人白白跑了一天、垂头丧气回城堡去的丧气模样，一面又看见自己坐在桌前，门闩插得严严实实，身旁放着一品脱酒，与一条鹿后腿相对而坐，那浓香四溢的肉汁随着刀子第三刀、第四刀地切下去，汩汩地淌了出来。

===Original===
The deer, as far as Thibault could calculate, was making for the bridge which crosses the Ourcq, between Noroy and Troesne. At the time of which we are now speaking there was a bridge spanning the river, formed of two joists and a few planks. As the river was very high and very rapid, Thibault decided that the deer would not attempt to ford it; so he hid himself behind a rock, within reach of the bridge, and waited.

It was not long before he saw the graceful head of the deer appear above the rock at some ten paces’ distance; the animal was bending its ears to the wind, in the endeavour to catch the sound of the enemy’s approach as it was borne along the breeze. Thibault, excited by this sudden appearance, rose from behind the rock, poised his boar-spear and sent it flying towards the animal.

The buck, with a single bound, reached the middle of the bridge, a second carried him on to the opposite bank, and a third bore him out of sight.

===Chinese===
据蒂博盘算，那头鹿是奔着诺鲁瓦（Noroy）与特雷斯纳（Troesne）之间横跨乌尔克河（the Ourcq）的那座桥去的。在我们此刻所述的那个年代，河上有一座桥，由两根托梁加几块厚木板搭成。因河水既深且急，蒂博断定那鹿不会涉水强渡；于是他躲到桥附近的一块岩石背后，静候其来。

不多时，他便瞧见那鹿秀气的头颅在岩石上方约十步开外探了出来；那畜生正把耳朵迎着风，想借着微风捕捉敌人逼近的声息。蒂博被这猝然出现的猎物激得心头一热，从岩石后一跃而起，端平野猪矛，朝那畜生飞掷过去。

那雄鹿一个纵身便跃到桥中央，第二纵到了对岸，第三纵便没了踪影。

===Original===
The boar-spear had passed within a foot of the animal, and had buried itself in the grass fifteen paces from where Thibault was standing. Never before had he been known to make such an unskilful throw; he, Thibault, of all the company who made the tour of France, the one known to be surest of his aim! Enraged with himself, therefore, he picked up his weapon, and bounded across the bridge with an agility equal to that of the deer.

Thibault knew the country quite as well as the animal he was pursuing, and so got ahead of the deer and once more concealed himself, this time behind a beech-tree, halfway up, and not too far from a little footpath.

The deer now passed so close to him, that Thibault hesitated as to whether it would not be better to knock the animal down with his boar-spear than to throw the weapon at it; but his hesitation did not last longer than a flash of lightning, for no lightning could be quicker than the animal itself, which was already twenty paces off when Thibault threw his boar-spear, but without better luck than the time before.

===Chinese===
那野猪矛擦着畜生身侧不过一尺之地飞过，扎进了蒂博所站之处十五步开外的草地里。他这还是头一回出手如此蹩脚；他蒂博其人，在那帮周游法兰西的同行之中，向来是以眼到手到、百发百中著称的！他因此恨得直咬牙，拾起兵器，纵身一跃过了桥，身手之捷竟不输那头鹿。

蒂博对这一带地形的熟稔，丝毫不亚于他所追的那头畜生，于是他又抢到了前头，再度藏起身子——这回是躲在一棵山毛榉树后，半山腰上，离一条羊肠小道不远。

那鹿这回擦着他身边经过，离得近得蒂博一时拿不定主意：是干脆拿野猪矛把畜生抡倒来得划算，还是照旧把它掷出去；可他这番迟疑连一道闪电的工夫都没撑到——因为天下没有哪道闪电比这畜生更快，待蒂博把野猪矛掷出去时，它早已跑出二十步开外，而这回的运气也不比上回强。

===Original===
And now the baying of the hounds was drawing nearer and nearer; another few minutes, and it would, he felt, be impossible for him to carry out his design. But in honour to his spirit of persistence, be it said, that in proportion as the difficulty increased, the greater became Thibault’s desire to get possession of the deer.

“I must have it, come what will,” he cried, “I must! and if there is a God who cares for the poor, I shall have satisfaction of this confounded Baron, who beat me as if I were a dog, but I am a man notwithstanding, and I am quite ready to prove the same to him.” And Thibault picked up his boar-spear and once more set off running. But it would appear that the good God whom he had just invoked, either had not heard him, or wished to drive him to extremities, for his third attempt had no greater success than the previous ones.

“By Heaven!” exclaimed Thibault, “God Almighty is assuredly deaf, it seems. Let the Devil then open his ears and hear me! In the name of God or of the Devil, I want you and I will have you, cursed animal!”

===Chinese===
此时猎犬的吠声已越来越近；他心知再过几分钟，这事便再也办不成了。但为这股子锲而不舍的韧劲计，应当说上一句：困难越是增大，蒂博要把那头鹿弄到手的欲望便越发强烈。

“我非要它不可，不论出什么事，”他嚷道，“我非要做到不可！要是有个看顾穷苦人的上帝，我定要找这该死的男爵算这笔账，他把我当狗一样打，可我到底是个汉子，我随时预备着向他证明这一点。”说罢蒂博拾起野猪矛，重又拔腿飞奔。可方才他所祈求的那位好上帝，似乎要么没听见他的祷告，要么有意把他逼到绝路上去，因为他这第三回出手，并不比前两回强多少。

“老天在上！”蒂博叫道，“全能上帝看样子是聋了。那就让魔鬼竖起耳朵来听我说吧！凭上帝也好，凭魔鬼也好，我要的是你，我非要你不可，你这该死的畜生！”

===Original===
Thibault had hardly finished this double blasphemy when the buck, doubling back, passed close to him for the fourth time, and disappeared among the bushes, but so quickly and unexpectedly, that Thibault had not even time to lift his boar-spear.

At that moment he heard the dogs so near him, that he deemed it would be imprudent to continue his pursuit. He looked round him, saw a thickly-leaved oak tree, threw his boar-spear into a bush, swarmed up the trunk, and hid himself among the foliage. He imagined, and with good reason, that since the deer had gone ahead again, the hunt would only pass by following on its track. The dogs had not lost the scent, in spite of the quarry’s doublings, and they were not likely to lose it now. Thibault had not been seated among the branches for above five minutes, when first the hounds came into sight, then the Baron, who in spite of his fifty-five years, headed the chase as if he had been a man of twenty. It must be added that the Lord of Vez was in a state of rage that we will not even endeavour to describe.

===Chinese===
蒂博这双料的亵渎之辞刚一出口，那头雄鹿竟又折了回来，第四次擦着他身边经过，一闪身便钻进了灌木丛里，来得之快、之突兀，蒂博连举起野猪矛的工夫都没有。

这时他听得猎犬的吠声已近在咫尺，心知再追下去未免太过冒失。他四下一望，瞧见一棵枝叶浓密的橡树，便把野猪矛往灌木丛里一丢，手脚并用攀上树干，藏到浓荫里去了。他有理由料定——而且理由十足——那鹿既然又跑到前头去了，围猎的人马不过循着它的踪迹从树下载过而已。那些猎犬尽管被猎物绕得团团转，却始终没丢了气味，眼下自然更不会丢。蒂博在枝桠间落座不过五分钟光景，先是猎犬们进了视野，继而是男爵——他虽已五十有五，冲在前头的劲头却像个二十岁的小伙子。还得补上一句：韦兹领主此刻那一腔怒火，我们就不必费笔墨去形容了。

===Original===
To lose four hours over a wretched deer and still to be running behind it! Such a thing had never happened to him before.

He stormed at his men, he whipped his dogs, and had so ploughed his horse’s sides with his spurs, that the thick coating of mud which covered his gaiters was reddened with blood.

On reaching the bridge over the Ourcq, however, there had been an interval of alleviation for the Baron, for the hounds had so unanimously taken up the scent, that the cloak which the wolf-hunter carried behind him would have sufficed to cover the whole pack as they crossed the bridge.

Indeed the Baron was so pleased, that he was not satisfied with humming a tirra-la, but, unslinging his hunting-horn he sounded it with his full lung-power, a thing which he only did on great occasions.

But, unfortunately, the joy of my Lord of Vez was destined to be short lived.

===Chinese===
为了一头区区的鹿白白耗费四个钟头，到头来还在它屁股后头撵！这种事他这辈子还是头一遭碰上。

他冲着手下的人破口大骂，拿鞭子抽他的猎犬，又拿马刺把坐骑的两肋戳得满是血痕，连护胫上那厚厚一层泥都被血染红了。

不过，一到乌尔克河的那座桥上，男爵总算得了片刻喘息之机，因为猎犬们异口同声地嗅上了气味，挤挤挨挨地涌过桥去，那阵势，猎人随身带着的那件斗篷往上一撒，几乎能把整群狗都罩在底下。

男爵一高兴，光是哼一支“嗒啦嗒”的小调还不解气，索性解下猎号，鼓足中气吹了一通——这种事他向来只在重大场合才做。

可惜韦兹领主这一欢喜注定好景不长。

===Original===
All of a sudden, just as the hounds, that were crying in concert in a way which more and more delighted the Baron’s ears, were passing under the tree where Thibault was perched, the whole pack came to a standstill, and every tongue was silenced as by enchantment. Marcotte, at his master’s command, dismounted to see if he could find any traces of the deer, the whippers-in ran up, and they and Marcotte looked about, but they could find nothing.

Then Engoulevent, who had set his heart on a view-halloo being sounded for the animal he had tracked down, joined the others, and he too began to search. Everyone was searching, calling out and trying to rouse the dogs, when above all the other voices, was heard, like the blast of a tempest, the voice of the Baron.

“Ten thousand devils!” he thundered.

===Chinese===
蓦地，正当那一群猎犬齐声合吠、越叫越叫男爵听得受用，正从蒂博所栖身的那棵树下经过时，整群狗忽然齐刷刷地站住了脚，每一条狗的舌头都像中了定身法似的一下子哑了。马尔科特（Marcotte）奉主人之命下马去查看能不能寻见鹿的踪迹，几名管犬役夫也奔了过来，他们和马尔科特四处张望，却什么也没找着。

这时，一心盼着能为自己所追踪到的那头畜生吹响一见猎物的号角的昂古勒旺（Engoulevent），也凑到众人一堆，跟着搜寻起来。众人正搜的搜、喊的喊、想法子哄狗起来，忽然之间，压过所有人声的，如同一阵暴风骤雨般，响起了男爵的嗓门。

“一万个小鬼！”他如雷轰响。

===Original===
“Have the dogs fallen into a pit-hole, Marcotte?”

“No, my Lord, they are here, but they are come to a check.”

“How! come to a standstill!” exclaimed the Baron.

“What is to be done, my Lord? I cannot understand what has happened, but such is the fact.”

“Come to a check!” again exclaimed the Baron, “come to a standstill, here, in the middle of the forest, here where there is no stream where the animal could have doubled, or rock for it to climb. You must be out of your mind, Marcotte!”

“I, out of my mind, my Lord?”

“Yes, you, you fool, as truly as your dogs are all worthless trash!”

===Chinese===
“狗是不是掉进地洞里去了，马尔科特？”

“没有，老爷，它们都在这儿，只是停下来了。”

“怎么！停下来了！”男爵叫道。

“怎么办呢，老爷？我也闹不明白出了什么事，可事实就是这样。”

“停下来了！”男爵又叫了一遍，“停下来了，在这密林当中，在这儿既没有畜生可以兜圈子的溪流，也没有它可以攀爬的岩石。马尔科特，你准是疯了！”

“我疯了，老爷？”

“不错，就是你，你这蠢货，就像你那群狗全是些没用的废物一样千真万确！”

===Original===
As a rule, Marcotte bore with admirable patience the insults which the Baron was in the habit of lavishing upon everybody about him at critical moments of the chase, but this word trash, applied to his dogs, was more than his habitual long-suffering could bear, and drawing himself up to his full height, he answered vehemently, “Trash, my Lord? my dogs worthless trash! dogs that have brought down an old wolf after such a furious run that the best horse in your stable was foundered! my dogs trash!”

“Yes, trash, worthless trash, I say it again, Marcotte. Only trash would stop at a check like that, after hunting one wretched buck so many hours on end.”

“My Lord,” answered Marcotte, in a tone of mingled dignity and sorrow, “My Lord, say that it is my fault, call me a fool, a blockhead, a scoundrel, a blackguard, an idiot; insult me in my own person, or in that of my wife, of my children, and it is nothing to me; but for the sake of all my past services to you, do not attack me in my office of chief pricker, do not insult your dogs.”

===Chinese===
往常每逢围猎的紧要关头，男爵总要把一通臭骂劈头盖脸地赏给身边的每一个人，马尔科特耐着性子受下来，那份涵养着实令人叹服。可这一个“废物”加在他那群猎犬头上，却超出他素日那份隐忍所能承受的极限，只见他把身子一挺，直直地立起来，激烈地回嘴道：“废物，老爷？我那群狗是没用的废物！那群把一头老狼追得如此凶悍、直追得您马厩里最好的马都跑脱了力的狗！我那群狗是废物！”

“不错，废物，没用的废物，我再说一遍，马尔科特。只有废物才会在连追一头区区小鹿追了这么多钟头之后，像这样莫名其妙地停下脚来。”

“老爷，”马尔科特以一种兼带尊严与悲凉的口吻答道，“老爷，您尽可说是我的不是，骂我蠢货、木头脑袋、恶棍、无赖、白痴；您辱骂我本人也好，辱骂我的妻子、我的孩子也好，我都毫不在乎；可念在我多年来为您效劳的份上，请不要在我这猎犬长一职上作践我，请不要辱骂您的猎犬。”

===Original===
“How do you account for their silence, then? tell me that! How do you account for it? I am quite willing to hear what you have to say, and I am listening.”

“I cannot explain it any more than you can, my Lord; the damned animal must have flown into the clouds or disappeared in the bowels of the earth.”

“What nonsense are you talking!” exclaimed the Baron—“do you want to make out that the deer has burrowed like a rabbit, or risen from the ground like a grouse?”

“My Lord, I meant it only as a manner of speech. What is a truth, what is the fact, is that there is some witchcraft behind all this. As sure as it is now daylight, my dogs, every one of them, lay down at the same moment, suddenly, without an instant hesitation. Ask anybody who was near them at the time. And now they are not even trying to recover the scent, but there they lie flat on the ground like so many stags in their lair. I ask you, is it natural?”

===Chinese===
“那么你又怎么解释它们一声不吭？你倒是说说看！你怎么解释？我倒很愿意听听你有什么话说，我洗耳恭听。”

“老爷，这事我跟您一样解释不来；那该死的畜生不是飞上了云端，便是钻进了地底。”

“你胡说八道些什么！”男爵嚷道——“你是想说那鹿像兔子一样钻了洞，还是像松鸡一样从地底下扑棱棱飞起来？”

“老爷，我不过打个比方罢了。千真万确、确凿无疑的是，这背后有邪术作祟。我可以拿现在这大天亮作保：我那群狗，每一条都在同一瞬间卧倒在地，突如其来，没有半分迟疑。您问问当时在跟前的人好了。如今它们连气味都懒得再找，就那么平平地趴在地上，活像一群卧在窝里的鹿。我问您，这合乎自然吗？”

===Original===
“Thrash them, man! thrash them, then,” cried the Baron, “flay the skin off their backs; there is nothing like it for driving out the evil spirit.”

And the Baron was going forward to emphasise with a few blows from his own whip the exorcisms which Marcotte, according to his orders, was distributing among the poor beasts, when Engoulevent, hat in hand, drew near to the Baron and timidly laid his hand on the horse’s bridle.

“My Lord,” said the keeper of the kennel, “I think I have just discovered a cuckoo in that tree who may perhaps be able to give us some explanation of what has happened.”

“What the devil are you talking about, with your cuckoo, you ape?” said the Baron. “If you wait a moment, you scamp, I will teach you how to come chaffing your master like that!”

And the Baron lifted his whip. But with all the heroism of a Spartan, Engoulevent lifted his arm above his head as a shield and continued:

“Strike, if you will, my Lord, but after that look up into this tree, and when your Lordship has seen the bird that is perched among the branches, I think you will be more ready to give me a crown than a blow.”

===Chinese===
“抽它们呀，伙计！抽它们呀，”男爵叫道，“把它们背上的皮都扒下来；驱邪没有比这更灵的法子了。”

男爵正要亲自上前，用自己的马鞭再补上几下，好给马尔科特依令正在往那群可怜畜生身上施展的驱邪仪式助助威——这时，昂古勒旺摘了帽子，凑到男爵跟前，怯生生地把一只手搭上了马笼头。

“老爷，”这位管犬役夫说道，“我想我刚在那棵树上发现了一只布谷鸟，说不定能给咱们方才发生的怪事作个交代。”

“你扯你那布谷鸟的什么鬼话，你这人猿？”男爵道，“你给我等着，你这泼皮，我这就教教你，看你还敢这般拿你主子开心不当！”

说着男爵便举起了马鞭。可昂古勒旺拿出斯巴达人式的英勇，把一条胳膊举过头顶权作盾牌，接着说道：

“您要打便打吧，老爷，可打完了请您抬头瞧瞧这棵树，等老爷您瞧见栖在枝桠间的这只鸟儿，我想您就更情愿赏我一枚埃居，而不是赏我一鞭子了。”

===Original===
And the good man pointed to the oak tree in which Thibault had taken refuge on hearing the huntsmen approach. He had climbed up from branch to branch and had finally hoisted himself on to the topmost one.

The Baron shaded his eyes with his hand, and, looking up, caught sight of Thibault.

“Well, here’s something mighty queer!” he cried, “It seems that in the forest of Villers-Cotterets the deer burrow like foxes, and men perch on trees like crows. However,” continued the worthy Baron, “we will see what sort of creature we have to deal with.” And putting his hand to his mouth, he halloed:

“Ho, there, my friend! would it be particularly disagreeable for you to give me ten minutes’ conversation?”

===Chinese===
说罢，这老实人指了指蒂博听见猎人走近便躲上去的那棵橡树。他已一根枝桠一根枝桠地爬上去，末了攀到了最高的一根。

男爵伸手遮在眼上挡住日光，抬头一望，瞧见了蒂博。

“嗬，这可真稀奇了！”他叫道，“看来在维莱-科特雷（Villers-Cotterets）这林子里，鹿像狐狸一样钻洞，人像乌鸦一样上树。也罢，”这位好男爵接着说，“咱们倒要看看，打交道的是个什么样的活物。”说罢他把手拢在嘴边，高声喊道：

“喂，上头那位朋友！跟你商量个事，下来陪我聊上十分钟，不会太让你为难吧？”

===Original===
But Thibault maintained the most profound silence.

“My Lord,” said Engoulevent, “if you like…,” and he made a sign to show that he was ready to climb the tree.

“No, no,” said the Baron, at the same time putting out his hand to hold him back.

“Ho, there, my friend!” repeated the Baron still without recognising Thibault, “will it please you to answer me, yes or no.”

He paused a second.

“I see, it is evidently, no; you pretend to be deaf, my friend; wait a moment, and I will get my speaking-trumpet,” and he held out his hand to Marcotte, who, guessing his intention, handed him his gun.

===Chinese===
可蒂博却保持着最深不可测的沉默。

“老爷，”昂古勒旺道，“您要是愿意……”说罢他做了个手势，表示自己愿意上树。

“不，不，”男爵说，同时伸手把他拦住。

“喂，上头那位朋友！”男爵又喊了一遍，仍旧没有认出是蒂博，“请你回我一句话好不好，行还是不行。”

他停了一停。

“我明白了，显然是不行；你这是装聋啊，朋友；等着，我去拿我的传声筒来。”说罢他向马尔科特伸出手去，马尔科特猜到他的心思，便把自己的猎枪递了过去。

===Original===
Thibault, who wished to put the huntsmen on the wrong scent, was meanwhile pretending to cut away the dead branches, and he put so much energy into this feigned occupation that he did not perceive the movement on the part of the Baron, or, if he saw, only took it as a menace, without attaching the importance to it which it merited.

The wolf-hunter waited for a little while to see if the answer would come, but as it did not, he pulled the trigger; the gun went off, and a branch was heard to crack.

The branch which cracked was the one on which Thibault was poised; the Baron was a fine shot and had broken it just between the trunk and the shoemaker’s foot.

===Chinese===
蒂博一心要把这帮猎人引上歧路，这时正装模作样地砍着枯枝，他在这假装的活计上使的力气太大，竟没留神男爵那边的动静；就算瞧见了，也只当是吓唬吓唬自己，没把它当一回事。

那猎狼人等了片刻，看有没有回音，见没有，便扣动了扳机；枪声一响，只听得一根枝桠“咔嚓”一声断了。

断的那根枝桠，正是蒂博脚踩着的那一根；男爵是个神枪手，这一枪不偏不倚，正打断在树干与鞋匠落脚处之间。

===Original===
Deprived of his support, Thibault fell, rolling from branch to banch. Fortunately the tree was thick, and the branches strong, so that his fall was broken and less rapid than it might have been, and he finally reached the ground, after many rebounds, without further ill consequences than a feeling of great fear and a few slight bruises on that part of his body which had first come in contact with the earth.

“By Beelzebub’s horns!” exclaimed the Baron, delighted with his own skill, “if it is not my joker of the morning! Ah! so, you scamp! did the discourse you had with my whip seem too short to you, that you are so anxious to take it up again where we left off?”

“Oh, as to that, I assure you it is not so, my Lord,” answered Thibault in a tone of the most perfect sincerity.

“So much the better for your skin, my good fellow. Well, and now tell me what you were doing up there, perched on the top of that oak-tree?”

“My Lord can see himself,” answered Thibault, pointing to a few dry twigs lying here and there on the ground, “I was cutting a little dry wood for fuel.”

===Chinese===
脚下一空，蒂博便栽了下来，从这根枝桠跌到那根枝桠。幸而这棵树枝叶繁密、枝干粗壮，把他的下坠之力层层卸去，落得比本来要慢，几经弹跳之后总算着了地，除了吓出一身冷汗、再加上最先着地的那处皮肉蹭破了几处轻伤之外，倒没落下别的毛病。

“凭别西卜的犄角起誓！”男爵叫道，为自己的枪法得意非凡，“这不是我早上那个说笑话的朋友吗！啊哈！原来如此，你这泼皮！早上我那马鞭给你上的课，你觉得太短了是不是，这么急着要从上次断的地方再续上一续？”

“噢，那个嘛，我向您保证并不是这么回事，老爷，”蒂博以一种再诚恳不过的口吻答道。

“那对你的皮肉可是再好不过了，我的好朋友。好了，这会儿你倒说说看，你蹲在那棵橡树顶上做什么来着？”

“老爷自己瞧得见，”蒂博答道，指了指东一截西一截散落在地上的几根枯枝，“我不过砍点干柴烧。”

===Original===
“Ah! I see. Now then, my good fellow, you will please tell us, without any beating about the bush, what has become of our deer.”

“By the devil, he ought to know, seeing that he has been perched up there so as not to lose any of its movements,” put in Marcotte.

“But I swear, my Lord,” said Thibault, “that I don’t know what it is you mean about this wretched buck.”

“Ah, I thought so,” cried Marcotte, delighted to divert his master’s ill-humour from himself, “he has not seen it, he has not seen the animal at all, he does not know what we mean by this wretched buck! But look here, my Lord, see, the marks on these leaves where the animal has bitten; it was just here that the dogs came to a full stop, and now, although the ground is good to show every mark, we can find no trace of the animal, for ten, twenty, or a hundred paces even?”

===Chinese===
“啊！我懂了。那么，我的好朋友，这会儿劳你大驾，痛痛快快地告诉我们，别绕弯子，我们那头鹿哪儿去了。”

“凭魔鬼起誓，他应该知道啊，瞧他蹲在上头那个架势，分明是为了把那畜生的一举一动都瞧在眼里。”马尔科特插嘴道。

“可我发誓，老爷，”蒂博说，“您说的这头该死的鹿，我压根儿不明白您指的是什么。”

“哈，我就知道！”马尔科特叫道，乐得把主子的火气从自己身上引开，“他没瞧见，他压根儿没瞧见这畜生，他不晓得我们说的这头该死的鹿是怎么回事！可是您瞧，老爷，您瞧，这些叶子上的齿痕，正是那畜生啃的；狗就是在这儿戛然停住的，可这会儿，尽管这地皮最能显出爪印，我们却连十步、二十步、哪怕一百步开外都寻不见那畜生的半点踪迹？”

===Original===
“You hear?” said the Baron, joining his words on to those of the pricker, “you were up there, and the deer here at your feet. It did not go by like a mouse without making any sound, and you did not see or hear. You must needs have seen or heard it!”

“He has killed the deer,” said Marcotte, “and hidden it away in a bush, that’s as clear as the day.”

“Oh, my Lord,” cried Thibault, who knew better than anybody else how mistaken the pricker was in making this accusation, “My Lord, by all the saints in paradise, I swear to you that I have not killed your deer; I swear it to you on the salvation of my soul, and, may I perish on the spot if I have given him even the slightest scratch. And besides, I could not have killed him without wounding him, and if I had wounded him, blood would have flowed; look, I pray you, sir,” continued Thibault turning to the pricker “and God be thanked, you will find no trace of blood. I, kill a poor beast! and, my God, with what? Where is my weapon? God knows I have no other weapon than this billhook. Look yourself, my Lord.”

===Chinese===
“你听见没有？”男爵接过猎犬长的话头说道，“你在上头，那鹿就在你脚边。它又不是像老鼠一样溜过去一点儿声响都没有，你怎么会既没瞧见也没听见。你不可能没瞧见、也没听见！”

“他把那鹿宰了，”马尔科特说，“藏到哪丛灌木里去了，这事儿明摆着跟青天一样。”

“噢，老爷，”蒂博叫道——他比谁都清楚这猎犬长的指控错得有多离谱，“老爷，凭天堂里众圣徒起誓，我向您发誓我没有宰您的鹿；我拿我灵魂的得救起誓，我若碰了它一根毫毛、哪怕抓出一点点擦痕，便叫我当场暴毙。况且，我要宰它便不能不伤它，伤了它便要流血；请您瞧瞧，先生，”蒂博转过头去对那猎犬长接着说道，“感谢上帝，您找不到一滴血的痕迹。我，宰一头可怜的畜生！再说了，我的老天，拿什么宰？我的兵器在哪儿？天晓得我除了这把钩镰，再没有别的家伙了。您自个儿瞧瞧吧，老爷。”

===Original===
But unfortunately for Thibault, he had hardly uttered these words, before Maître Engoulevent, who had been prowling about for some minutes past, reappeared, carrying the boar-spear which Thibault had thrown into one of the bushes before climbing up the tree.

He handed the weapon to the Baron.

There was no doubt about it—Engoulevent was Thibault’s evil genius.

===Chinese===
可蒂博倒霉就倒霉在，这话才出口，昂古勒旺师傅（Maître Engoulevent）——这几分钟一直在四周转悠——便又冒了出来，手里拎着的正是蒂博上树之前丢进灌木丛里的那杆野猪矛。

他把这兵器递给了男爵。

毫无疑问——昂古勒旺正是蒂博命中的克星。

## IX The Wolf-Leader / IX 狼之领主

===Original===
Thibault, fleeing from before Madame Polet’s threats and her farm servants’ weapons, turned instinctively towards the forest, thinking to take shelter within it, should he chance to come across one of the enemy, for he knew that no one would venture to follow him there, for fear of any lurking dangers. Not that Thibault had much to fear, whatever kind of enemy he met, now that he was armed with the diabolical power which he had received from the wolf. He had only to send them where he had sent the widow’s pig, and he was sure of being rid of them. Nevertheless, conscious of a certain tightening of the heart when from time to time the thought of Marcotte came back to him, he acknowledged to himself that, however anxious to be rid of them, one could not send men to the devil quite as readily as one sent pigs.

===Chinese===
蒂博逃离了波莱太太（Madame Polet）的威胁和她那些长工的棍棒，本能地朝森林那边逃去，心想万一撞上哪个仇家，便好躲进林子里藏身，因为他知道，谁也不敢冒着林中潜伏的种种凶险追他进去。其实，如今蒂博已握有那头狼赋予他的魔鬼之力，无论碰上什么样的仇敌，他也没什么好怕的。他只消把来人打发到寡妇那口猪去的地方，便可稳稳除掉他们。然而，每当马尔科特（Marcotte）的影子偶尔浮上心头，他胸口仍不免一阵发紧，于是他暗自承认：哪怕再巴不得摆脱这些人，把活人打发去见魔鬼，到底不能像打发一口猪那样爽快。

===Original===
While thus reflecting on the terrible power he possessed, and looking back at intervals to see if there were any immediate need to put it into use, Thibault, by the time night fell, had reached the rear of Pisseleu. It was an autumn night, dark and stormy, with a wind that tore the yellowing leaves from the trees, and wandered through the forest ways with melancholy sighs and moanings. These funereal voices of the wind were interrupted from time to time by the hooting of the owls, which sounded like the cries of lost travellers, hailing one another. But all these sounds were familiar to Thibault and made very little impression upon him. Moreover he had taken the precaution, on first entering the forest, of cutting a stick, four feet long, from a chestnut tree, and adept as he was with the quarterstaff, he was ready, armed thus, to withstand the attack of any four men. So he entered the forest with all boldness of heart, at the spot which is known to this day as the Wolf’s Heath. He had been walking for some minutes along a dark and narrow glade, cursing as he went the foolish whims of women, who, for no reason whatever, preferred a weak and timid child to a brave, strong, full grown man, when all of a sudden, at some few paces behind him, he heard a crackling among the leaves. He turned and the first thing he could distinguish in the darkness was the glowing light in a pair of eyes which shone like live coals. Then, looking more closely, and forcing his eyes, so to speak, to penetrate the gloom, he saw that a great wolf was following him, step by step. But it was not the wolf that he had entertained in his hut; that was black, while this was a reddish-brown. There was no mistaking one for the other, either as to colour or size. As Thibault had no reason to suppose that all the wolves he came across would be animated by such benevolent feelings towards him as the first with which he had had dealings, he grasped his quarterstaff in both hands, and began twirling it about to make sure he had not forgotten the knack of using it. But to his great surprise the wolf went on trotting quietly behind him, without evincing any hostile intention, pausing when he paused, and going on again when he did, only now and then giving a howl as if to summon reinforcements. Thibault was not altogether without uneasiness as regards these occasional howls, and presently he became aware of two other bright spots of light in front of him, shining at intervals through the darkness which was growing thicker and thicker. Holding his stick up in readiness to hit, he went forward towards these two lights, which remained stationary, and as he did so, his foot seemed to stumble against something lying across the path﻿ ﻿… it was another wolf. Not pausing to reflect whether it might not be unwise now to attack the first wolf, Thibault brought down his staff, giving the fellow a violent blow on the head. The animal uttered a howl of pain, then shaking his ears like a dog that has been beaten by its master, began walking on in front of the shoemaker. Thibault then turned to see what had become of the first wolf: it was still following him, still keeping step with him. Bringing his eyes back again to the front, he now perceived that a third wolf was walking alongside to the right, and turning instinctively to the left, saw a fourth flanking him on that side too. Before he had gone a mile, a dozen of the animals had formed a circle round him. The situation was critical, and Thibault was fully conscious of its gravity. At first he tried to sing, hoping that the sound of the human voice might frighten away the animals; but the expedient was vain. Not a single animal swerved from its place in the circle, which was as exactly formed as if drawn with compasses. Then he thought he would climb up into the first thick-leaved tree he came to, and there wait for daylight; but on further deliberation, he decided that the wisest course was to try to get home, as the wolves, in spite of their number, still appeared as well intentioned as when there was only one. It would be time enough to climb up into a tree when they began to show signs of any change of behaviour towards him.

===Chinese===
蒂博一边这样盘算着自己所掌握的那股可怕力量，一边不时回头张望，看看眼下有没有必要动用它。待到夜幕降临，他已走到了皮瑟勒（Pisseleu）的后头。那是一个秋夜，天色阴沉，风雨欲来，风把树上发黄的叶子一片片扯落，在林间的道上四处游荡，发出哀伤的叹息与呻吟。这些送葬似的悲声，不时被猫头鹰的啼叫打断，听去就像迷路的旅人此呼彼应。可这些声响蒂博样样听惯，对他几乎不起什么作用。况且他一进林子便防了一手，从一棵栗树上砍下一根四尺来长的木棍——蒂博是耍短棍的好手，有此物在手，便是四条汉子齐上也应付得来。于是他大着胆子，在一处至今仍叫作狼荒原（the Wolf’s Heath）的地方进了林子。他沿着一条幽暗狭窄的林间空地走了几分钟，一边走一边咒骂女人们那些糊涂古怪的脾气——她们竟会毫无道理地宁可挑一个孱弱胆怯的孩子，也不挑一个勇武强壮、正当年纪的汉子。猛然间，他听见身后几步开外的落叶堆里响起一阵窸窸窣窣的响动。他一回头，黑地里头一样辨得清的，便是一双亮得像烧红的炭似的眼珠子。继而再凝神细看，硬逼着两眼往那团幽暗里钻，这才看出是一头大狼，正一步一步地跟在他身后。可这并非是他在小屋里招待过的那头狼；那一头是黑的，这一头却是赤褐色。无论看毛色还是看个头，两者都绝不会认混。蒂博没有理由以为，他所遇到的每一头狼，都会像头一回打交道的那一头一样对他怀着好意，于是他双手攥紧短棍，把它抡了几圈，看看自己是否还使得惯这兵器。孰料叫他又惊又奇的是，那狼竟仍旧不紧不慢地小跑着跟在后头，毫无敌意：他停它也停，他走它也走，只是偶尔嚎上一声，像是在召唤援兵。这几声时有时无的嚎叫叫蒂博心里很不踏实，不多时他又发觉，前方也有两点亮光，在越来越浓的夜色里一闪一闪地透出来。他把棍子高高擎起，预备着下手，朝那两点纹丝不动的亮光走去——脚底下却像绊到了横在路上的什么东西……原来又是一头狼。蒂博也顾不上想一想此刻再招惹头一头狼是否失策，抡起棍子便砸，结结实实地敲在那畜生的脑门上。那畜生痛得嚎叫了一声，接着便像一条挨了主人打的狗那样甩了甩耳朵，走到鞋匠前头去开路了。蒂博回过头去看头一头狼怎么样了：它仍旧跟着他，仍旧与他亦步亦趋。再把目光收回到前头，这回他瞧见第三头狼正顺着右边与他并排走着，出于本能往左边一扭头，又看见第四头狼也在那一边与他两相呼应。还没走出一英里，便有十二头这样的畜生把他围成了一个圈。情势已是万分凶险，蒂博也清清楚楚地觉出了其中的厉害。起先他想唱歌，指望人声能把这群畜生吓跑；可这一招全然无用。没有一头畜生挪动它在圈中的位置，那圈子规规矩矩，活像拿圆规画出来的一般。继而又想，索性爬上头一棵枝叶茂密的大树，在上面捱到天亮算了；可再一盘算，觉得最妥当的还是设法回家去，因为这群狼虽多，瞧着倒还和起初只有一头时一样心存善意。等它们真露出变脸的苗头再爬树也不迟。

===Original===
At the same time we are bound to add that Thibault was so disquieted in mind and that he had reached his own door before he knew where he was, he did not at first recognise his own house. But a still greater surprise awaited him, for the wolves who were in front now respectfully drew back into two lines, sitting up on their hind legs and making a lane for him to pass along. Thibault did not waste time in stopping to thank them for this act of courtesy, but dashed into the house, banging the door to after him. Having firmly shut and bolted the door, he pushed the great chest against it, that it might be better able to resist any assault that might be made upon it. Then he flung himself into a chair, and began at length to find himself able to breathe more freely.

As soon as he was somewhat recovered, he went and peeped through the little window that looked out on the forest. A row of gleaming eyes assured him that far from having retired, the wolves had arranged themselves symmetrically in file in front of his dwelling.

To anyone else the mere proximity of the animals would have been most alarming, but, Thibault who shortly before, had been obliged to walk escorted by this terrible troop, found comfort in the thought that a wall, however thin, now separated him from his formidable companions.

===Chinese===
与此同时，我们必须补上一句：蒂博心里慌得厉害，竟一路走到了自家门口还不自知，起初连自己的屋子都没认出来。可还有一桩更大的意外在等着他呢——原来走在前头的那几头狼，这会儿竟恭恭敬敬地退开，分作两厢，用后腿坐起身来，给他让出一条道来。蒂博也顾不上停下来答谢这番礼数，一头冲进屋里，反手把门砰地关上。他插好门闩，又把那口大木箱抵在门上，好叫这门更禁得住可能到来的冲撞。这才一屁股坐进椅子里，这才觉得自己总算能喘过一口气来了。

等稍稍缓过劲来，他便走到那扇朝着森林的小窗前，从缝里往外张望。一排闪闪发亮的眼珠告诉他，这些狼非但没有散去，反倒整整齐齐地在他屋前列成一排。

换了旁人，单是这些畜生近在咫尺，便已吓得魂飞魄散；可蒂博方才不得不由这一队可怕的队伍簇拥着走了一路，此刻一想到不管多薄的一堵墙，总算把他和这帮厉害的同伴隔开了，倒还觉得几分安慰。

===Original===
Thibault lit his little iron lamp and put it on the table; drew the scattered wood-ashes of his hearth together and threw on them a bundle of chips, and then made a good fire, hoping that the reflection of the blaze would frighten away the wolves. But Thibault’s wolves were evidently wolves of a special sort, accustomed to fire, for they did not budge an inch from the post they had taken up. The state of uneasiness he was in prevented Thibault from sleeping, and directly dawn broke, he was able to look out and count them. They seemed, just as on the night before, to be waiting, some seated, some lying down, others sleeping or walking up and down like sentinels. But at length, as the last star melted away, drowned in the waves of purple light ebbing up from the east, all the wolves with one accord rose, and uttering the mournful howl with which animals of darkness are wont to salute the day, they dispersed in various directions and disappeared. Thibault was now able to sit down and think over the misadventure of the previous day, and he began by asking himself how it was that the mistress of the Mill had not preferred him to his cousin Landry. Was he no longer the handsome Thibault, or had some disadvantageous change come over his personal appearance? There was only one way of ascertaining whether this was so or not, namely, by consulting his mirror. So he took down the fragment of looking-glass hanging over the chimneypiece, and carried it towards the light, smiling to himself the while like a vain woman. But he had hardly given the first glance at himself in the mirror, before he uttered a cry, half of astonishment, half of horror. True, he was still the handsome Thibault, but the one red hair, thanks to the hasty wishes which had so imprudently escaped him, had now grown to a regular lock of hair, of a colour and brilliancy that vied with the brightest flames upon his hearth.

===Chinese===
蒂博点亮他那盏小铁灯，搁在桌上；又把炉灶里散落的草木灰归拢到一处，往上面扔了一把劈柴，把火生得旺旺的，指望那火光的映照能把这些狼吓走。可蒂博这帮狼显然与众不同，是见过火的，一寸也不肯挪动它们所占的位置。他这一夜心里七上八下，合不得眼，天刚一亮便往外看，数起它们来。它们和昨夜一样，像是在等候什么：有的坐着，有的趴着，有的睡着，还有的像哨兵一样来回踱步。可终于，当最后一颗星子融化在自东方涌上来的紫红色光浪里、沉没下去，所有的狼一齐站起身来，发出那种黑暗中的畜生惯于用来迎接白昼的哀嚎，随即四散奔开，没了踪影。蒂博这才坐下来，回想昨日那桩倒霉事，头一个涌上心头的疑问便是：磨坊女主人怎么就不肯要他、偏看中他表兄朗德里（Landry）呢？莫非他不再是那个英俊的蒂博了，又或者他的容貌有了什么不妙的变化？要弄清这一点，只有一条法子，那便是照一照镜子。于是他把挂在壁炉上方的破镜片摘下来，端到光亮处，一边独自笑吟吟的，像个爱慕虚荣的女人。可他对着镜子才瞧了头一眼，便失声叫了起来，那叫声一半是惊奇，一半是骇惧。不错，他还是那个英俊的蒂博，可那一根红发（red hair），拜他一时嘴快、冒冒失失许下的那几个愿所赐，如今竟长成了整整一绺头发，那颜色、那光泽，竟与炉膛里最旺的火苗不相上下。

===Original===
His forehead grew cold with sweat. Knowing, however, that all attempts to pluck it out or cut it off would be futile, he made up his mind to make the best of the matter as it stood, and in future to forbear as far as possible from framing any wishes. The best thing was to put out of his mind all the ambitious desires that had worked so fatally for him, and go back to his humble trade. So Thibault sat down and tried to work, but he had no heart for the job. In vain he tried to remember the carols he had been in the habit of singing in the happier days when the beech and the birch shaped themselves so quickly beneath his fingers; his tools lay untouched for hours together. He pondered over matters, asking himself whether it was not a miserable thing to be sweating one’s heart out merely for the privilege of leading a painful and wretched existence, when, by judiciously directing one’s wishes one might so easily attain to happiness. Formerly, even the preparation of his frugal meal had been an agreeable distraction, but it was so no longer; when hunger seized him and he was forced to eat his piece of black bread, he did it with a feeling of repugnance, and the envy, which had hitherto been nothing more than a vague aspiration after ease and comfort, was now developed into a blind and violent hatred towards his fellow creatures.

Still the day, long as it seemed to Thibault, passed away like all its fellows. When twilight fell, he went outside and sat down on the bench which he had made himself and placed in front of the door, and there he remained, lost in gloomy reflections. Scarcely had the shadows begun to darken, before a wolf emerged from the underwood, and, as on the previous evening, went and lay down at a short distance from the house. As on the evening before, this wolf was followed by a second, by a third, in short by the whole pack, and once more they all took up their respective posts preparatory to the night’s watch. As soon as Thibault saw the third wolf appear, he went indoors and barricaded himself in as carefully as the evening before; but this evening he was even more unhappy and low spirited, and felt that he had not the strength to keep awake all night. So he lighted his fire, and piled it up in such a way that it would last till the morning, and throwing himself on his bed, fell fast asleep. When he awoke, it was broad daylight, the sun having risen some hours before. Its rays fell in many colours on the quivering autumn leaves, dyeing them with a thousand shades of gold and purple.

Thibault ran to the window, the wolves had disappeared, leaving behind only the mark of where their bodies had lain on the dew-covered grass.

===Chinese===
他额上沁出了一层冷汗。可他也晓得，无论拔也好、剪也好，都无济于事，便索性打定主意，既来之则安之，往后尽量别再许什么愿。最要紧的，是把那些害苦了他的勃勃野心从脑子里统统赶出去，回头老老实实干他那卑微的老本行。于是蒂博坐下来想做活，可手底下全无心思。他试着回想从前那些快活日子里常唱的圣诞歌——那时候，山毛榉和桦树在他的指头底下那么麻利地就成了形——可任他怎么想也想不起来；他的家什一连几个钟头搁着不动。他翻来覆去地寻思，这世上还有比这更憋屈的事么：拼死拼活地熬着，图的不过是过一种又苦又穷的日子；而只要把愿许得精明些，便轻而易举地够得到幸福。从前，便是张罗他那顿粗茶淡饭，也算一桩开心的消遣，如今却再也不是了；饿上来、不得不啃那块黑面包的时候，他满心都是嫌恶，而那股嫉妒之心——原先不过是图个安逸舒坦的一团模糊念头——如今已滋长成一股又盲目又凶狠的对同类的仇恨。

话虽如此，这一天在蒂博看来长得没了期，到底也和别的日子一样地过去了。暮色一落，他便走到屋外，在自个儿做的、安在门口的那条长凳上坐下，闷头想他的心事。影影绰绰的暮色才一浓起来，便有一头狼从灌木丛里钻出来，像昨儿傍晚那样，到离屋子不远的地方趴下了。也像昨儿傍晚那样，这头狼后头跟着第二头、第三头，末了是整整一群，它们各自又占了昨夜的那些位置，预备着守夜。蒂博一瞧见第三头狼露面，便钻进屋里，像头天晚上一样仔仔细细地把门户关严实了；可这一晚他更加愁闷、更加丧气，觉得自己没有那股子熬夜的精气神了。于是他把火生着，添足了柴，摆弄成一烧能烧到大天亮的架势，往床上一倒，便沉沉睡死了过去。等他睁开眼，已是白亮亮的白天，太阳出来已有几个钟头了。阳光五光十色地落在颤动的秋叶上，把它们染成千种金、万种紫。

蒂博跑到窗前一看，狼群已经散了，只留下沾满露水的草地上它们身子压出的那一摊摊印子。

===Original===
Next evening they again congregated before his dwelling; but he was now growing gradually accustomed to their presence, and had come to the conclusion that his relations with the large black wolf had somehow awakened sympathetic feelings towards him in all other individuals of the same species, and he determined to find out, once for all, what their designs towards him really were. Accordingly, thrusting a freshly sharpened billhook into his belt, and taking his boar-spear in his hand, the shoemaker opened his door and walked resolutely out to face them. Having half expected that they would spring upon him, he was greatly surprised to see them begin to wag their tails like so many dogs on seeing their master approach. Their greetings were so expressive of friendliness, that Thibault even ventured to stroke one or two of them on the back, which they not only allowed him to do, but actually gave signs of the greatest pleasure at being thus noticed.

“Oh! ho!” muttered Thibault, whose wandering imagination always went ahead at a gallop, “if these queer friends of mine are as obedient as they are gentle, why, here I am, the owner of a pack unequalled by any my Lord Baron has ever possessed, and I shall have no difficulty whatever now in dining on venison whenever the fancy so takes me.”

===Chinese===
第三天傍晚，狼群又在他屋前聚齐了；可这会儿他已慢慢习惯了它们的在场，也断定了：他与那头大黑狼的瓜葛，不知怎的，已唤起了同类之中其余每一个个体对他的好感。他打定主意，索性一次性弄明白，它们到底打他什么主意。于是鞋匠把一把刚磨快的钩镰（billhook）插进腰带，手里攥着他的野猪矛（boar-spear），推开门，毅然走了出去，迎面朝它们走去。他原有一半以为它们会扑上来的，及至瞧见它们竟像一群瞧见主人走近的狗那样摇起尾巴来，不由得又惊又喜。它们那番亲热劲儿实在溢于言表，蒂博甚至大着胆子伸手去摸了一两头狼的背，它们不但任他摸，还分明显出受了这番抬举便欢喜得不得了的模样。

“噢！嚯！”蒂博喃喃自语——他那胡思乱想的脑子向来是一溜小跑地往前赶——“要是我这些古怪朋友跟它们脾气一样地听话，嘿，那我不就成了一个狗群的主人么，男爵老爷（my Lord Baron）养过的哪一群也比不上；往后我什么时候想吃鹿肉打牙祭，岂不是再没有半点难处了。”

===Original===
He had hardly said the words, when four of the strongest and most alert of the four-footed beasts separated themselves from the others and galloped off into the forest. A few minutes later a howl was heard, sounding from the depths of the underwood, and half an hour afterwards one of the wolves reappeared dragging with it a fine kid which left behind it a long trail of blood on the grass. The wolf laid the animal at Thibault’s feet, who delighted beyond measure at seeing his wishes, not only accomplished, but forestalled, broke up the kid, giving each of the wolves an equal share, and keeping the back and haunches for himself. Then with the gesture of an Emperor, which showed that he now at last understood the position he held, he ordered the wolves away until the morrow.

Early next morning, before the day broke, he went off to Villers-Cotterets, and at the price of a couple of crowns, the Innkeeper of the Boule-d’Or, took the two haunches off his hands.

The following day, it was half of a boar that Thibault conveyed to the Innkeeper, and it was not long before he became the latter’s chief purveyor.

===Chinese===
话音未落，那群四足畜生里最壮实、最机警的四个便离了群，撒开蹄子朝林子里飞奔而去。几分钟后，灌木丛深处传来一声嚎叫，又过了半个钟头，一头狼重新露面，身后拖着一头肥嫩的小山羊，草地上留下一道长长的血迹。那狼把猎物放在蒂博脚边，蒂博喜出望外，他的愿非但被应验，而且是被预先揣度着办到了，便把小山羊分了分，每头狼各得一份，一样多，背脊和两条后腿则留给自己。随后他做了一个帝王般的手势——可见他到此刻总算明白了自己所处的地位——下令狼群退下，直到第二天再来。

第二天一大早，天还没亮，他便动身去了维莱-科特雷（Villers-Cotterets），以两枚埃居（crown）的价钱，把那两条后腿脱手给了金球客栈（the Boule-d’Or）的老板。

又过了一天，蒂博送到那老板手里的，是半头野猪；没过多久，他便成了这位老板最主要的供应户。

===Original===
Thibault, taking a taste for this sort of business, now passed his whole day hanging about the taverns, and gave no more thought to the making of shoes. One or two of his acquaintances began to make fun of his red lock, for however assiduously he covered it with the rest of his hair, it always found a way of getting through the curls that hid it, and making itself visible. But Thibault soon gave it plainly to be understood that he would take no joking about the unfortunate disfigurement.

Meanwhile, as ill luck would have it, the Duke of Orleans and Madame de Montesson came to spend a few days at Villers-Cotterets. This was a fresh incentive to Thibault’s madly ambitious spirit. All the fine and beautiful ladies and all the gay young lords from the neighbouring estates, the Montbretons, the Montesquious, the Courvals, hastened to Villers-Cotterets. The ladies brought their richest attire, the young lords their most elegant costumes. The Baron’s hunting-horn resounded through the forest louder and gayer than ever. Graceful amazons and dashing cavaliers, in red coats laced with gold, passed like radiant visions, as they were borne along on their magnificent English horses, illuminating the sombre depths of the wood like brilliant flashes of light.

===Chinese===
蒂博对这桩营生渐渐上了瘾，如今整日整日地泡在酒馆附近厮混，再不肯费心思做鞋了。有一两个相熟的人开始拿他那绺红发（red lock）打趣——因为无论他怎样费尽心机地拿别的头发盖住它，那绺头发总有办法从遮掩它的发卷里钻出来，自管自地招摇。可蒂博很快就叫人明白：这桩不幸的破相之事，谁要拿来取笑，他是概不答应的。

偏偏这当口祸不单行，奥尔良公爵（the Duke of Orleans）和蒙特森夫人（Madame de Montesson）到维莱-科特雷来小住几日。这对蒂博那颗疯狂勃勃的野心，又添了一把新柴。四邻八乡的贵妇小姐、风流少年——蒙布雷东家（the Montbretons）、孟德斯鸠家（the Montesquious）、库瓦尔家（the Courvals）——都争先恐后地赶到维莱-科特雷来。太太小姐们带来了最华贵的衣裙，年轻老爷们带来了最考究的行头。男爵的猎号在林子里响得比往日更欢更闹。英姿飒爽的骑装女郎和风流倜傥的少年骑士，一色金线绦子的红猎装，骑着他们那神骏的英国马，如同一道道光彩夺目的幻影一掠而过，像一道道璀璨的闪光，把密林深处最幽暗的角落都照亮了。

===Original===
In the evening it was different; then all this aristocratic company assembled for feasting and dancing, or at other times drove out in beautiful gilt carriages bedizened with coats of arms of every colour.

Thibault always took his stand in the front rank of the lookers-on, gazing with avidity on these clouds of satin and lace, which lifted now and then to disclose the delicate ankles encased in their fine silk stockings, and the little shoes with their red heels. Thus the whole cavalcade swept past in front of the astonished peasantry, leaving a faint exhalation of scent and powder and delicate perfumes. And then Thibault would ask himself why he was not one of those young lords in their embroidered coats; why he had not one of these beautiful women in their rustling satins for his mistress. Then his thoughts would turn to Agnelette and Madame Polet, and he saw them just as they were, the one a poor little peasant girl, the other nothing more than the owner of a rustic mill.

===Chinese===
到了晚上又自不同；这一群贵族男女聚在一处饮宴跳舞，又或乘着金碧辉煌的马车出游，那马车上缀满了五颜六色的纹章。

蒂博每次都挤在围观人群的最前头，贪婪地盯着那一团团锦缎与花边——那些裙裾时不时被掀起一角，露出裹在细丝袜里的一截秀美脚踝，以及那小小红跟的绣鞋。这一整队人马便这样从瞠目结舌的乡下人跟前浩浩荡荡地掠过，留下一缕淡淡的香粉和名贵香水的气息。每到这时，蒂博便要问自己：为什么他不是那些身着绣花礼服的少年老爷中的一个？为什么那些窸窣作响的锦缎里头的佳人，竟没有一位是他的相好？继而他的念头又转到阿涅莱特（Agnelette）和波莱太太身上，照她们本来的样子看她们了：一个不过是个可怜的乡下小姑娘，另一个也不过是一座乡野磨坊的主人罢了。

===Original===
But it was when he was walking home at night through the forest, accompanied by his pack of wolves, which, from the moment the night fell and he set foot inside the forest, no more thought of leaving him than the King’s bodyguard would dream of leaving their Royal master, that his broodings took their most disastrous turn. Surrounded by the temptations which now assailed him, it was only what was to be expected that Thibault who had already gone so far in the direction of evil, should break away from what little good was still left in him, losing even the very remembrance of having once led an honest life. What were the few paltry crowns that the Landlord of the Boule-d’Or gave him in payment for the game which his good friends the wolves procured for him? Saved up for months, even for years, they would still be insufficient to satisfy a single one of the humblest of the desires which kept tormenting his brain. It would be scarcely safe to say that Thibault, who had first wished for a haunch of the Baron’s buck, then for Agnelette’s heart, and then for the widow Polet’s mill, would now be satisfied even with the Castle at Oigny or Longpont, to such extravagant issues had his ambition been excited by those dainty feet, those trim ankles, those exquisite scents exhaled from all those velvet and satin gowns.

At last one day he said to himself definitely that it would be the veriest folly to go on living his poor life when a power so tremendous as he now possessed, was at his disposal. From that moment he made up his mind that, no matter if his hair should grow as red as the crown of fire which is seen at night hanging over the great chimney at the glass works of Saint Gobain, he would exercise this power of his to the accomplishing of the most high-flown of his ambitions.

===Chinese===
可他的邪念想得最是要命的，还是夜里穿过森林走回家的路上——那时他的狼群一路相随，自天一黑、他踏进林子那一刻起，它们便再没想过要离开他，正像国王的御前侍卫决不会做梦丢下他们王室的主子不管一样。四面八方都是眼下向他涌来的种种诱惑，蒂博既已在邪路上走了这么远，会挣脱他心里残存的那一点点善、甚至连曾经清清白白地过日子这件事都忘个干净，原也在意料之中。金球客栈的老板为那些好狼朋友替他弄来的野味付给他的那几个可怜埃居，又算得了什么呢？便是攒上几个月、甚至几年，也还远远填不满那一直折磨他脑子的种种欲念之中最微不足道的一个。蒂博头一个愿，许的是男爵那头鹿的一条后腿，接着许的是阿涅莱特的一颗心，再接着许的是波莱寡妇的磨坊，如今谁也不敢打包票说，便是把瓦尼（Oigny）或龙蓬（Longpont）的城堡给了他，他便心满意足了——他的野心已被那一只只秀气的脚、一截截纤细的脚踝、那一身身天鹅绒与锦缎里透出的种种幽香，撩拨到了如此荒唐离谱的地步。

终于有一天，他斩钉截铁地对自己说：既然手里握着这么一股通天的力量听凭驱遣，再这么窝窝囊囊地过他那穷日子，未免太愚蠢了。从那一刻起他便拿定主意：哪怕他的头发会长得像圣戈班（Saint Gobain）玻璃厂那根大烟囱上夜里看得见的那顶火冠一样红，他也要把这股力量使出来，去成就他那最登峰造极的野心。

## XXII Thibault's Last Wish / XXII 蒂博的最后一个愿望

===Original===
Urged in her flight by a hideous terror, and anxious to reach the village where she had left her husband with all speed possible, Agnelette, for the very reason that she was running so hastily, was forced by her failing breath, to pause at intervals along the way. During these short spaces of rest, she endeavoured to reason with herself, trying to convince herself of the folly of attaching importance to words which could have no power in themselves, and which were dictated by jealousy and hatred, words which had by now been scattered to the winds; but notwithstanding all her mental arguments, she had no sooner regained her breath than she started off again at the same precipitate pace, for she felt she should know no peace until she had seen her husband again. Best part of her way led through the forest, and near the wildest and most solitary enclosures, but she gave no thought to the wolves, which were the terror of every town and village within ten miles round. Only one fear possessed her, that of coming across her husband's dead body. More than once, as her foot struck against a stone or a branch, her heart stopped beating, and she felt as if her last breath had been drawn, while a sharp cold seemed to enter her very vitals, her hair stood on end and her face grew wet with perspiration. At last, at the end of the long path she had been traversing, arched over by the trees, she saw ahead of her, a vista of open country lying bathed in the soft silver light of the moon. As she emerged from the gloom into the light, a man, who had been concealed behind a bush in the hollow lying between the forest and the open country, sprang in front of her and took her in his arms.

"Ah! ah!" he said, laughing, "and where are you off to, Madame, at this hour of the night, and at this pace too?" Agnelette recognised her husband.

"Etienne! dear, dear Etienne," cried the young woman, throwing her arms round his neck. "How thankful I am to see you again, and to find you alive and well! Oh, my God, I thank Thee!"

"What, did you think, you poor little Agnelette," said Engoulevent, "that Thibault and his wolves had been making their dinner of me?"

"Ah! do not even speak of Thibault, Etienne! let us fly, dear one, fly to where there are houses!"

===Chinese===
阿涅莱特（Agnelette）被一种可怖的恐惧驱赶着拼命奔逃，急于以最快的速度赶回她丢下丈夫的那个村庄，可正因为她跑得太急，渐渐不继的气息迫使她不得不沿路时时停下来喘息。在这些短暂的歇息里，她竭力说服自己，劝自己不必把那些话当真——那些话本身并无什么法力，不过是出于嫉妒与仇恨才说出口的，到如今早已被风吹散了；然而无论她在心里怎样开解自己，一口气才喘过来，便又以方才那般不顾命的速度重新跑了起来，因为她觉得自己不见到丈夫便一刻不得安宁。她这一路大半要穿过森林，经过那些最为荒僻、最无人迹的林间围场，可她全然顾不上狼群——要知道那狼群是方圆十英里内每一座城镇村庄的祸患。唯有一样恐惧攫住了她，那便是撞见丈夫的尸首。不止一回，她的脚绊在一块石头或一截枯枝上，她的心便骤然停跳，仿佛那一口气便是她最后的一口气，一阵尖锐的寒意直透五脏六腑，头发倒竖，满脸冷汗涔涔。终于，在她一路奔来的那条长长的小径尽头——头顶上方枝叶交织如穹顶——她望见前方豁然开朗，一片原野沐浴在月亮那柔和的银辉之中。她方从幽暗中踏入这银光之下，一个汉子便从森林与原野之间那道洼地里的一丛灌木后头蹿了出来，张开双臂将她一把搂住。

“啊！啊！”他笑着说道，“您这是要往哪儿去呀，太太，这般夜深时分，还跑得这般急？”阿涅莱特认出了她的丈夫。

“艾蒂安！亲爱的、亲爱的艾蒂安（Etienne）！”少妇叫出声来，双臂搂住了他的脖颈，“能再见到你、见你好端端地活着，我心里多欢喜啊！噢，我的上帝，我感谢您！”

“怎么，你以为，我可怜的小阿涅莱特，”昂古勒旺（Engoulevent）说道，“蒂博（Thibault）和他那一群狼已经把我当晚饭吃了？”

“啊！连蒂博这个名字也别提，艾蒂安！咱们快逃吧，亲爱的，逃到有房屋的地方去！”

===Original===
The young huntsman laughed again. "Well, now then, you will make all the gossips of Préciamont and Vez declare that a husband is of no use at all, not even to restore his wife's courage."

"You are right, Etienne; but although I have just had the courage to come through these great dreadful woods, now that I have you with me and should feel reassured, I tremble with fear, and yet I know not why."

"What has happened to you? Come, tell me all about it," said Etienne, giving his wife a kiss. Then Agnelette told him how she had been attacked by the wolf, how Thibault had rescued her from its claws, and what had passed between them afterwards. Engoulevent listened with the greatest attention.

"Listen," he said to Agnelette, "I am going to take you home and shut you up carefully with the grandmother, so that no harm may come to you; and then I shall ride over and tell my lord of Vez where Thibault has taken up his quarters."

"Oh! no, no!" cried Agnelette, "you would have to ride through the forest, and there is no knowing what danger you might run."

"I will make a detour," said Etienne, "I can go round by Croyolles and Value instead of crossing the forest."

===Chinese===
年轻的猎人又笑了起来。“好啦好啦，你这话要是让普雷西亚蒙（Préciamont）和韦兹（Vez）那帮长舌妇人听见了，保准都说：做丈夫的真是一点用处也没有，连给妻子壮壮胆都办不到。”

“你说得对，艾蒂安；可虽说我方才有那胆子独个儿穿过这一大片可怕的树林，这会儿有你在身边、本该宽了心才是，我却还是怕得直打哆嗦，而我自己也说不清是为什么。”

“你到底遇上什么事了？来，原原本本地讲给我听。”艾蒂安说着，亲了妻子一下。于是阿涅莱特便把那头狼如何扑袭她、蒂博如何从狼爪下将她救出、以及之后两人之间所发生的一切，一一讲给他听。昂古勒旺凝神细听，一字不落。

“你听我说，”他对阿涅莱特说道，“我先把你送回家，把你和奶奶一块儿严严实实地安顿好，保准叫你毫发无损；然后我骑马过去一趟，把蒂博的藏身之处禀报给韦兹领主（the lord of Vez）。”

“噢！不，不！”阿涅莱特叫道，“那你可就得穿过森林了，天知道你会碰上什么样的凶险呢。”

“我绕道走，”艾蒂安说，“我可以兜个圈子，打克鲁瓦约尔（Croyolles）和瓦吕（Value）那边过去，不必穿过森林。”

===Original===
Agnelette sighed and shook her head, but made no further resistance; she knew that Engoulevent would not give in on this matter, and she reserved her strength wherewith to renew her entreaties when she was once indoors.

And in truth, the young huntsman only considered that he was doing his duty, for a great battue had been arranged for the next day in a part of the forest on the further side from that on which Agnelette had met Thibault. Etienne, therefore, was bound to go without delay and report to his master the whereabouts of the Wolf-leader. There was not too much of the night left for the work of rearranging for the morrow's battue.

As they drew near Préciamont, Agnelette, who had not spoken for a while, decided that she had, during her silence, amassed a sufficient number of reasons to justify her in beginning her solicitations afresh, which she did with even more earnestness than she had put into her former arguments. She reminded Etienne that Thibault, even though he might be a werewolf, had, so far from hurting her, actually saved her life; and that after all, he had not abused his power when he had her in it, but had allowed her to leave him and rejoin her husband. And after that, to betray where he was to his mortal enemy, the Lord of Vez, was not performing a duty, but committing an act of treachery; and Thibault, who would certainly get wind of this treachery, would never under similar circumstances show mercy to anyone again. Agnelette became quite eloquent as she pleaded Thibault's cause. But, when marrying Engoulevent, she had made no more secret of her former engagement to the shoemaker than she had of this last interview with him, and however perfect a confidence he had in his wife, Engoulevent was nevertheless not unsusceptible to jealousy. More than that, there existed an old grudge between the two men, ever since the day when Engoulevent had spied out Thibault in his tree, and his boar-spear in a neighbouring bush. So he stood his ground, and though listening to Agnelette, continued to walk briskly towards Préciamont. And so arguing together, and each insisting that he or she was in the right, they came to within a stone's throw of the first forest-fences. To protect themselves as far as possible from Thibault's sudden and unexpected assaults, the peasants had instituted patrol parties, who mounted guard at night as in times of war. Etienne and Agnelette were so preoccupied with their discussion, that they did not hear the call of "Who goes there!" from the sentinel behind the hedge, and went walking on in the direction of the village. The sentinel, seeing something moving in the darkness which to his prepossessed imagination appeared to be a monstrous form of some kind, and hearing no answer to his challenge, he prepared to shoot. Looking up at that moment, the young huntsman suddenly caught sight of the sentinel, as the moonlight shone on the barrel of his gun. Calling out "Friend," he threw himself in front of Agnelette, flinging his arms round her, so as to make a shield of his body. But at the same instant the gun went off, and the unfortunate Etienne, giving one last sigh, fell forward without a groan against the wife he was clasping in his arms. The bullet had pierced his heart. When the people of Préciamont, on hearing the gun shot, came running up to the spot, they found Engoulevent dead, and Agnelette lying unconscious beside her husband. They carried her to her grandmother's, but she only came to her senses to fall into a state of despair which bordered on delirium, and which at last became almost madness. She accused herself of her husband's death, called him by name, begged the invisible spirits, which seemed to haunt her, even in the short intervals of slumber which her excited state of brain made possible, to have mercy upon him. She called Thibault's name, and addressed such heartbroken supplications to him that those about her were moved to tears. By degrees, in spite of the incoherence of her words, the real facts became evident, and it grew to be generally understood that the Wolf-leader was in some way accountable for the unhappy accident which had caused poor Etienne's death. The common enemy was therefore accused of having cast a spell over the two unfortunate young creatures, and the animosity felt towards the former shoemaker became intensified.

===Chinese===
阿涅莱特叹了口气，摇了摇头，却没再阻拦；她知道昂古勒旺在这件事上绝不会让步，便留着力气，等进了屋再重新苦苦央求他。

说实在的，这年轻猎人不过是觉得自个儿在尽本分罢了，因为次日已安排了一场大围猎，地点就在森林那一边——与阿涅莱特撞见蒂博的那一头相隔着整片林子。艾蒂安因此责无旁贷，非得立刻去将狼之领主（the Wolf-leader）的下落禀报主人不可。要重新安排明日围猎的事宜，所剩的这一夜光阴已不算多了。

两人将近普雷西亚蒙时，沉默了好一阵子的阿涅莱特，自以为在这一阵沉默里已攒足了充足的理由，足以叫她重又开始央求起来——这一回央求得比方才更加恳切。她提醒艾蒂安说，蒂博即便当真是个狼人，非但未曾伤她分毫，反倒救了她的性命；再者说，当她落在他手里之时，他也并未仗势欺凌于她，而是放她走、让她回到丈夫身边。既然如此，转头便去向他的死仇韦兹领主告发他的藏身之处，那便不是在尽什么本分，而是在行一桩背信弃义之事；而蒂博一旦嗅出这桩背信弃义的风声（他必定会嗅出来的），日后便是再遇着同样的情形，也断断不会再发什么善心了。阿涅莱特替蒂博辩白起来，竟说得头头是道、颇为动人。可她当年嫁给昂古勒旺时，对从前与那鞋匠的婚约并未讳莫如深，对这最后一回的相会也不曾有半点隐瞒，因此昂古勒旺对妻子纵有万分的信任，却也不是全然不会吃醋的。更何况，这两个男人之间本就有一笔旧账，打从那一日昂古勒旺在树上窥破了蒂博、又在他旁边的灌木丛里搜出那杆野猪矛（boar-spear）起，这笔账便记下了。于是他寸步不让，一面听着阿涅莱特说话，一面脚下不停，仍是大步流星地朝普雷西亚蒙走去。两人就这样一路争执着，各人都坚持自个儿有理，不知不觉已走到离第一道林边栅栏不过一掷之遥的地方。庄户人为了尽可能防备蒂博猝不及防的袭击，早已组织起了巡逻队，夜间轮班守望，一如战时那般。艾蒂安和阿涅莱特两人正全神贯注地争辩，竟没听见树篱后头那哨兵一声“口令！”的喝问，径直朝村庄的方向走去。那哨兵瞧见黑暗中有什么东西在移动——在他那先入为主、疑神疑鬼的想象里，那东西仿佛是一头什么形状可怖的怪物——又听得一声喝问无人回应，便端枪待发。就在这一刹那，年轻的猎人猛一抬头，借着月光照在枪管上的一闪，忽然瞧见了那哨兵。他高喊一声“朋友”，便纵身扑到阿涅莱特身前，张开双臂将她紧紧搂住，拿自己的身子作她的一面盾牌。可就在同一瞬间，枪声响了，可怜的艾蒂安只发出最后一声叹息，便一声不哼地朝他所紧搂着的妻子身上扑倒下去。子弹洞穿了他的心脏。普雷西亚蒙的人们听见枪声，纷纷奔到出事地点，只见昂古勒旺已然断了气，阿涅莱特昏死在丈夫身旁。众人将她抬到她祖母家中，可她一醒过来便陷入了近乎谵妄的绝望之中，到最后几乎与疯癫无异。她痛责自己害了丈夫的性命，一遍又一遍地唤着他的名字，央求那些仿佛缠着她不放的无形幽魂——哪怕在她那亢奋异常的脑子所能容许的片刻浅眠之中，那些幽魂也似来缠扰——对它发发慈悲。她唤着蒂博的名字，向他说出那样肝肠寸断的哀求之辞，引得身旁之人无不落泪。渐渐地，纵然她的话前言不搭后语，事情的真相却一点点显出了轮廓，众人慢慢地都明白了过来：狼之领主与那桩害了可怜艾蒂安性命的不幸意外，多少脱不开干系。于是这个众矢之的便被指控对这一对不幸的年轻人施了邪术，乡人们对这个昔日鞋匠的仇恨，愈发炽烈了。

===Original===
In vain doctors were sent for from Villers-Cotterets and Ferté-Milon, Agnelette became worse and worse; her strength was rapidly failing; her voice, after the first few days, grew feebler, her breath shorter, although her delirium was as violent as ever, and everything, even the silence on the doctors' part, led to the belief that poor Agnelette would soon follow her husband to the grave. The voice of the old blind woman alone seemed to have any power to allay the fever. When she heard her grandmother speaking, she grew calmer, the haggard staring eyes grew softer and suffused with tears; she would pass her hand over her forehead as if to drive away some haunting thought, and a sorrowful wandering smile would pass across her lips.

One evening, towards night, her slumber seemed to be more agitated and distressed than usual. The hut, feebly lit by a little copper lamp, was in semidarkness; the grandmother sat by the hearth, with that immobility of countenance under which peasants and savages hide their strongest feelings. At the foot of the bed on which Agnelette lay, so worn and white that, had it not been for the regular rise and fall of her bosom with its troubled breathing, you might have taken her for dead, knelt one of the women, whom the Baron was paying to attend upon the widow of his young huntsman, engaged in telling her beads; the other was silently spinning with her distaff. All at once, the sick woman, who for some minutes past had been shivering at intervals, seemed to be fighting against some horrible dream, and gave a piercing cry of anguish. At that moment the door burst open, a man seemingly encircled by flames, rushed into the room, leapt to Agnelette's bed, clasped the dying woman in his arms, pressed his lips upon her forehead, uttering cries of sorrow, then, rushing to another door which gave on to the open country, opened it and disappeared. The apparition had come and gone so quickly that it seemed almost like an hallucination, and as if Agnelette were endeavouring to repulse some invisible object as she cried out, "Take him away! take him away!" But the two watchers had seen the man and had recognised Thibault, and there was a clamouring outside, in the midst of which the name of Thibault could be distinguished. Soon the clamour came nearer to Agnelette's hut, and those who were uttering the cries ere long appeared on the threshold; they were in pursuit of the Wolf-leader. Thibault had been seen prowling in the neighbourhood of the hut, and the villagers, warned of this by their sentinels, had armed themselves with pitchforks and sticks preparatory to giving him chase.
